What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop focusing on this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch on my face. I see it in the mirror and in photos, and it makes one side of my face look old and beat-up to me. Other people tell me they barely notice anything, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about this patch on your face throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s on my mind a lot. I keep catching it in the mirror or thinking about how it looked in a photo, that rough, kind of sunken patch. To me it makes one side of my face look old and beat-up, even though people say they barely notice it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the patch on your face interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. I’ve been late getting out the door for construction jobs because I get stuck checking it or trying to make it look less noticeable. And I’ve started avoiding site photos. I’ve also skipped some family game nights because I just don’t want people looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern only about the appearance of this patch on your face or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s really just that patch on my face. I don’t spend much time worrying about other parts of my body. It’s that rough, sunken-looking spot that I keep seeing in the mirror and in photos, like it makes one side of my face look old or beat-up, even though people tell me they barely notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repetitive, like touching or picking at the patch on your face, in response to your concerns about its appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Before work, or before going to my sister’s game night, I’ll get stuck checking it in the bathroom mirror. I turn my face different ways under the light to see how bad it looks. I’ll rub at the spot too, like that’s going to change anything. Sometimes I wear my hat low, or if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior related to the patch on your face is causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, it’s not just bothering me in my head — it’s making me late leaving for construction jobs, and I’ve started dodging site photos. I’ve even skipped some family game nights because I don’t want everyone looking at my face. So yeah, it’s causing me stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or rubbing the patch on your face if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I tell myself I’m just going to look once, but then I end up turning my face under the light, checking it again, rubbing at the spot. Especially before work or going to my sister’s game night. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or start thinking of a reason not to go. It’s hard to just stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking or rubbing the patch on your face in response to an anxiety or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s pretty much it. I get this anxious feeling like I have to look at it, like I can’t just leave it alone. Before work or before my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or rubbing the patch on your face is something you feel compelled to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t really want to do it, but it feels like I have to. Before work, or if I’m going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at that spot. And if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or start making an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or rub the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I feel like if I don’t check it, I might go out and everyone will notice it. Like before work or my sister’s game night, I keep looking in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under the light, rubbing at it. If it seems too obvious, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just come up with a reason not to go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s hard to just stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking or rubbing the patch on your face in response to an anxiety or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s pretty much it. I get this anxious feeling like I have to look at it, like I can’t just leave it alone. Before work or before my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or rubbing the patch on your face is something you feel compelled to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t really want to do it, but it feels like I have to. Before work, or if I’m going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at that spot. And if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or start making an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or rub the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I feel like if I don’t check it, I might go out and everyone will notice it. Like before work or my sister’s game night, I keep looking in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under the light, rubbing at it. If it seems too obvious, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just come up with a reason not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the patch on your face is a ritual you have to complete before you can feel okay about going out?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is. Before I go to work or over to my sister’s game night, I end up in the bathroom checking it over and over, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. I don’t really feel okay leaving until I’ve done that. And if it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop focusing on this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch on my face. I see it in the mirror and in photos, and it makes one side of my face look old and beat-up to me. Other people tell me they barely notice anything, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about this patch on your face throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s on my mind a lot. I keep catching it in the mirror or thinking about how it looked in a photo, that rough, kind of sunken patch. To me it makes one side of my face look old and beat-up, even though people say they barely notice it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the patch on your face interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. I’ve been late getting out the door for construction jobs because I get stuck checking it or trying to make it look less noticeable. And I’ve started avoiding site photos. I’ve also skipped some family game nights because I just don’t want people looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern only about the appearance of this patch on your face or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s really just that patch on my face. I don’t spend much time worrying about other parts of my body. It’s that rough, sunken-looking spot that I keep seeing in the mirror and in photos, like it makes one side of my face look old or beat-up, even though people tell me they barely notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repetitive, like touching or picking at the patch on your face, in response to your concerns about its appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Before work, or before going to my sister’s game night, I’ll get stuck checking it in the bathroom mirror. I turn my face different ways under the light to see how bad it looks. I’ll rub at the spot too, like that’s going to change anything. Sometimes I wear my hat low, or if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior related to the patch on your face is causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, it’s not just bothering me in my head — it’s making me late leaving for construction jobs, and I’ve started dodging site photos. I’ve even skipped some family game nights because I don’t want everyone looking at my face. So yeah, it’s causing me stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or rubbing the patch on your face if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I tell myself I’m just going to look once, but then I end up turning my face under the light, checking it again, rubbing at the spot. Especially before work or going to my sister’s game night. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or start thinking of a reason not to go. It’s hard to just stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking or rubbing the patch on your face in response to an anxiety or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s pretty much it. I get this anxious feeling like I have to look at it, like I can’t just leave it alone. Before work or before my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or rubbing the patch on your face is something you feel compelled to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t really want to do it, but it feels like I have to. Before work, or if I’m going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at that spot. And if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or start making an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or rub the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I feel like if I don’t check it, I might go out and everyone will notice it. Like before work or my sister’s game night, I keep looking in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under the light, rubbing at it. If it seems too obvious, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just come up with a reason not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the patch on your face is a ritual you have to complete before you can feel okay about going out?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is. Before I go to work or over to my sister’s game night, I end up in the bathroom checking it over and over, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. I don’t really feel okay leaving until I’ve done that. And if it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check the patch on your face if you didn't think it looked bad?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much, no. It’s really when I think it looks bad or too obvious that I get stuck checking it. Like before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the mirror, turn my face in different lights, rub at the spot… and if I still think it shows, I’ll wear my hat low or make an excuse not to go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop focusing on this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch on my face. I see it in the mirror and in photos, and it makes one side of my face look old and beat-up to me. Other people tell me they barely notice anything, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about this patch on your face throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s on my mind a lot. I keep catching it in the mirror or thinking about how it looked in a photo, that rough, kind of sunken patch. To me it makes one side of my face look old and beat-up, even though people say they barely notice it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the patch on your face interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. I’ve been late getting out the door for construction jobs because I get stuck checking it or trying to make it look less noticeable. And I’ve started avoiding site photos. I’ve also skipped some family game nights because I just don’t want people looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern only about the appearance of this patch on your face or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s really just that patch on my face. I don’t spend much time worrying about other parts of my body. It’s that rough, sunken-looking spot that I keep seeing in the mirror and in photos, like it makes one side of my face look old or beat-up, even though people tell me they barely notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repetitive, like touching or picking at the patch on your face, in response to your concerns about its appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Before work, or before going to my sister’s game night, I’ll get stuck checking it in the bathroom mirror. I turn my face different ways under the light to see how bad it looks. I’ll rub at the spot too, like that’s going to change anything. Sometimes I wear my hat low, or if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior related to the patch on your face is causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, it’s not just bothering me in my head — it’s making me late leaving for construction jobs, and I’ve started dodging site photos. I’ve even skipped some family game nights because I don’t want everyone looking at my face. So yeah, it’s causing me stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or rubbing the patch on your face if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I tell myself I’m just going to look once, but then I end up turning my face under the light, checking it again, rubbing at the spot. Especially before work or going to my sister’s game night. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or start thinking of a reason not to go. It’s hard to just stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking or rubbing the patch on your face in response to an anxiety or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s pretty much it. I get this anxious feeling like I have to look at it, like I can’t just leave it alone. Before work or before my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or rubbing the patch on your face is something you feel compelled to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t really want to do it, but it feels like I have to. Before work, or if I’m going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at that spot. And if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or start making an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or rub the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I feel like if I don’t check it, I might go out and everyone will notice it. Like before work or my sister’s game night, I keep looking in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under the light, rubbing at it. If it seems too obvious, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just come up with a reason not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the patch on your face is a ritual you have to complete before you can feel okay about going out?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is. Before I go to work or over to my sister’s game night, I end up in the bathroom checking it over and over, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. I don’t really feel okay leaving until I’ve done that. And if it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check the patch on your face if you didn't think it looked bad?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much, no. It’s really when I think it looks bad or too obvious that I get stuck checking it. Like before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the mirror, turn my face in different lights, rub at the spot… and if I still think it shows, I’ll wear my hat low or make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check the patch on your face driven more by the possibility that it might look bad to others, or by an internal feeling that you just have to check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more about thinking it might look bad to other people. Like, before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking it in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at it. If I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ve been late getting out the door for construction jobs because I get stuck checking it or trying to make it look less noticeable. And I’ve started avoiding site photos. I’ve also skipped some family game nights because I just don’t want people looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern only about the appearance of this patch on your face or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s really just that patch on my face. I don’t spend much time worrying about other parts of my body. It’s that rough, sunken-looking spot that I keep seeing in the mirror and in photos, like it makes one side of my face look old or beat-up, even though people tell me they barely notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repetitive, like touching or picking at the patch on your face, in response to your concerns about its appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Before work, or before going to my sister’s game night, I’ll get stuck checking it in the bathroom mirror. I turn my face different ways under the light to see how bad it looks. I’ll rub at the spot too, like that’s going to change anything. Sometimes I wear my hat low, or if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior related to the patch on your face is causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, it’s not just bothering me in my head — it’s making me late leaving for construction jobs, and I’ve started dodging site photos. I’ve even skipped some family game nights because I don’t want everyone looking at my face. So yeah, it’s causing me stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or rubbing the patch on your face if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I tell myself I’m just going to look once, but then I end up turning my face under the light, checking it again, rubbing at the spot. Especially before work or going to my sister’s game night. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or start thinking of a reason not to go. It’s hard to just stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking or rubbing the patch on your face in response to an anxiety or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s pretty much it. I get this anxious feeling like I have to look at it, like I can’t just leave it alone. Before work or before my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or rubbing the patch on your face is something you feel compelled to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t really want to do it, but it feels like I have to. Before work, or if I’m going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at that spot. And if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or start making an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or rub the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I feel like if I don’t check it, I might go out and everyone will notice it. Like before work or my sister’s game night, I keep looking in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under the light, rubbing at it. If it seems too obvious, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just come up with a reason not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the patch on your face is a ritual you have to complete before you can feel okay about going out?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is. Before I go to work or over to my sister’s game night, I end up in the bathroom checking it over and over, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. I don’t really feel okay leaving until I’ve done that. And if it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check the patch on your face if you didn't think it looked bad?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much, no. It’s really when I think it looks bad or too obvious that I get stuck checking it. Like before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the mirror, turn my face in different lights, rub at the spot… and if I still think it shows, I’ll wear my hat low or make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check the patch on your face driven more by the possibility that it might look bad to others, or by an internal feeling that you just have to check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more about thinking it might look bad to other people. Like, before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking it in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at it. If I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check the patch on your face driven by the fact that you think it's actually flawed, or is it more that you just feel compelled to check it regardless of how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s because I think it’s really flawed. I see this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch on my face, especially in photos or in the mirror, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up. People tell me they barely notice it, but I can’t stop seeing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don’t really want to do it, but it feels like I have to. Like, before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking it in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at it. If I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check the patch on your face driven by the fact that you think it's actually flawed, or is it more that you just feel compelled to check it regardless of how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s because I think it’s really flawed. People tell me they barely notice it, but I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check the patch on your face if someone you trust told you it looked perfectly fine?\n\nPatient: Even if someone I trust said it looked fine, I’d probably still check it. Sometimes I wear my hat low, or if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior related to the patch on your face is causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, it’s not just bothering me in my head — it’s making me late leaving for construction jobs, and I’ve started dodging site photos. I’ve even skipped some family game nights because I don’t want everyone looking at my face. So yeah, it’s causing me stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or rubbing the patch on your face if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I tell myself I’m just going to look once, but then I end up turning my face under the light, checking it again, rubbing at the spot. Especially before work or going to my sister’s game night. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or start thinking of a reason not to go. It’s hard to just stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking or rubbing the patch on your face in response to an anxiety or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s pretty much it. I get this anxious feeling like I have to look at it, like I can’t just leave it alone. Before work or before my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or rubbing the patch on your face is something you feel compelled to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t really want to do it, but it feels like I have to. Before work, or if I’m going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at that spot. And if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or start making an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or rub the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I feel like if I don’t check it, I might go out and everyone will notice it. Like before work or my sister’s game night, I keep looking in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under the light, rubbing at it. If it seems too obvious, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just come up with a reason not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the patch on your face is a ritual you have to complete before you can feel okay about going out?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is. Before I go to work or over to my sister’s game night, I end up in the bathroom checking it over and over, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. I don’t really feel okay leaving until I’ve done that. And if it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check the patch on your face if you didn't think it looked bad?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much, no. It’s really when I think it looks bad or too obvious that I get stuck checking it. Like before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the mirror, turn my face in different lights, rub at the spot… and if I still think it shows, I’ll wear my hat low or make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check the patch on your face driven more by the possibility that it might look bad to others, or by an internal feeling that you just have to check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more about thinking it might look bad to other people. Like, before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking it in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at it. If I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check the patch on your face driven by the fact that you think it's actually flawed, or is it more that you just feel compelled to check it regardless of how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s because I think it’s really flawed. I see this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch on my face, especially in photos or in the mirror, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up. People tell me they barely notice it, but I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check the patch on your face if someone you trust told you it looked perfectly fine?\n\nPatient: Even if someone I trust said it looked fine, I’d probably still check it. I might feel better for a minute, but before work or before going to my sister’s game night, I’d end up back in the bathroom mirror, turning my face in the light and rubbing at the spot. If it still looked too obvious to me, I’d wear my hat low or sometimes just not go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is actually flawed, or do you just feel compelled to keep checking it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s actually flawed. I mean, I know people tell me they barely notice anything, but when I look in the mirror or see it in photos, I see this rough, kind of sunken patch, and it makes that side of my face look old and beat-up. That’s what keeps pulling me back to check it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the mirror, turn my face in different lights, rub at the spot… and if I still think it shows, I’ll wear my hat low or make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check the patch on your face driven more by the possibility that it might look bad to others, or by an internal feeling that you just have to check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more about thinking it might look bad to other people. Like, before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking it in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at it. If I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check the patch on your face driven by the fact that you think it's actually flawed, or is it more that you just feel compelled to check it regardless of how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s because I think it’s really flawed. I see this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch on my face, especially in photos or in the mirror, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up. People tell me they barely notice it, but I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check the patch on your face if someone you trust told you it looked perfectly fine?\n\nPatient: Even if someone I trust said it looked fine, I’d probably still check it. I might feel better for a minute, but before work or before going to my sister’s game night, I’d end up back in the bathroom mirror, turning my face in the light and rubbing at the spot. If it still looked too obvious to me, I’d wear my hat low or sometimes just not go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is actually flawed, or do you just feel compelled to keep checking it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s actually flawed. I mean, I know people tell me they barely notice anything, but when I look in the mirror or see it in photos, I see this rough, kind of sunken patch, and it makes that side of my face look old and beat-up. That’s what keeps pulling me back to check it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to check the patch on your face if you were completely alone and nobody would see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would still check it. Even if nobody was going to see me, I’d still end up looking in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights and rubbing at that spot. It’s like I need to see how bad it looks to me. Before work or going somewhere, like my sister’s game night, it gets worse, and if it looks too obvious I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop focusing on this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch on my face. I see it in the mirror and in photos, and it makes one side of my face look old and beat-up to me. Other people tell me they barely notice anything, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about this patch on your face throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s on my mind a lot. I keep catching it in the mirror or thinking about how it looked in a photo, that rough, kind of sunken patch. To me it makes one side of my face look old and beat-up, even though people say they barely notice it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the patch on your face interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. I’ve been late getting out the door for construction jobs because I get stuck checking it or trying to make it look less noticeable. And I’ve started avoiding site photos. I’ve also skipped some family game nights because I just don’t want people looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern only about the appearance of this patch on your face or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s really just that patch on my face. I don’t spend much time worrying about other parts of my body. It’s that rough, sunken-looking spot that I keep seeing in the mirror and in photos, like it makes one side of my face look old or beat-up, even though people tell me they barely notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repetitive, like touching or picking at the patch on your face, in response to your concerns about its appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Before work, or before going to my sister’s game night, I’ll get stuck checking it in the bathroom mirror. I turn my face different ways under the light to see how bad it looks. I’ll rub at the spot too, like that’s going to change anything. Sometimes I wear my hat low, or if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior related to the patch on your face is causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, it’s not just bothering me in my head — it’s making me late leaving for construction jobs, and I’ve started dodging site photos. I’ve even skipped some family game nights because I don’t want everyone looking at my face. So yeah, it’s causing me stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or rubbing the patch on your face if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I tell myself I’m just going to look once, but then I end up turning my face under the light, checking it again, rubbing at the spot. Especially before work or going to my sister’s game night. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or start thinking of a reason not to go. It’s hard to just stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking or rubbing the patch on your face in response to an anxiety or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s pretty much it. I get this anxious feeling like I have to look at it, like I can’t just leave it alone. Before work or before my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or rubbing the patch on your face is something you feel compelled to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t really want to do it, but it feels like I have to. Before work, or if I’m going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at that spot. And if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or start making an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or rub the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I feel like if I don’t check it, I might go out and everyone will notice it. Like before work or my sister’s game night, I keep looking in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under the light, rubbing at it. If it seems too obvious, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just come up with a reason not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the patch on your face is a ritual you have to complete before you can feel okay about going out?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is. Before I go to work or over to my sister’s game night, I end up in the bathroom checking it over and over, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. I don’t really feel okay leaving until I’ve done that. And if it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check the patch on your face if you didn't think it looked bad?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much, no. It’s really when I think it looks bad or too obvious that I get stuck checking it. Like before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the mirror, turn my face in different lights, rub at the spot… and if I still think it shows, I’ll wear my hat low or make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check the patch on your face driven more by the possibility that it might look bad to others, or by an internal feeling that you just have to check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more about thinking it might look bad to other people. Like, before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking it in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at it. If I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check the patch on your face driven by the fact that you think it's actually flawed, or is it more that you just feel compelled to check it regardless of how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s because I think it’s really flawed. I see this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch on my face, especially in photos or in the mirror, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up. People tell me they barely notice it, but I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check the patch on your face if someone you trust told you it looked perfectly fine?\n\nPatient: Even if someone I trust said it looked fine, I’d probably still check it. I might feel better for a minute, but before work or before going to my sister’s game night, I’d end up back in the bathroom mirror, turning my face in the light and rubbing at the spot. If it still looked too obvious to me, I’d wear my hat low or sometimes just not go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is actually flawed, or do you just feel compelled to keep checking it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s actually flawed. I mean, I know people tell me they barely notice anything, but when I look in the mirror or see it in photos, I see this rough, kind of sunken patch, and it makes that side of my face look old and beat-up. That’s what keeps pulling me back to check it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to check the patch on your face if you were completely alone and nobody would see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would still check it. Before work or going somewhere, like my sister’s game night, it gets worse, and if it looks too obvious I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check the patch is to fix or improve how it looks, or is it just something you feel you must do?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but mostly it feels like I have to do it. Before work, or if I’m going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, turning my face in different light and rubbing at the spot like maybe I can make it look better. And if I decide it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. That’s what keeps pulling me back to check it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to check the patch on your face if you were completely alone and nobody would see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would still check it. Even if nobody was going to see me, I’d still end up looking in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights and rubbing at that spot. It’s like I need to see how bad it looks to me. Before work or going somewhere, like my sister’s game night, it gets worse, and if it looks too obvious I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check the patch is to fix or improve how it looks, or is it just something you feel you must do?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but mostly it feels like I have to do it. Before work, or if I’m going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, turning my face in different light and rubbing at the spot like maybe I can make it look better. And if I decide it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop focusing on this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch on my face. I see it in the mirror and in photos, and it makes one side of my face look old and beat-up to me. Other people tell me they barely notice anything, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about this patch on your face throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s on my mind a lot. I keep catching it in the mirror or thinking about how it looked in a photo, that rough, kind of sunken patch. To me it makes one side of my face look old and beat-up, even though people say they barely notice it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the patch on your face interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. I’ve been late getting out the door for construction jobs because I get stuck checking it or trying to make it look less noticeable. And I’ve started avoiding site photos. I’ve also skipped some family game nights because I just don’t want people looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern only about the appearance of this patch on your face or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s really just that patch on my face. I don’t spend much time worrying about other parts of my body. It’s that rough, sunken-looking spot that I keep seeing in the mirror and in photos, like it makes one side of my face look old or beat-up, even though people tell me they barely notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repetitive, like touching or picking at the patch on your face, in response to your concerns about its appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Before work, or before going to my sister’s game night, I’ll get stuck checking it in the bathroom mirror. I turn my face different ways under the light to see how bad it looks. I’ll rub at the spot too, like that’s going to change anything. Sometimes I wear my hat low, or if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior related to the patch on your face is causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, it’s not just bothering me in my head — it’s making me late leaving for construction jobs, and I’ve started dodging site photos. I’ve even skipped some family game nights because I don’t want everyone looking at my face. So yeah, it’s causing me stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or rubbing the patch on your face if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I tell myself I’m just going to look once, but then I end up turning my face under the light, checking it again, rubbing at the spot. Especially before work or going to my sister’s game night. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or start thinking of a reason not to go. It’s hard to just stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking or rubbing the patch on your face in response to an anxiety or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s pretty much it. I get this anxious feeling like I have to look at it, like I can’t just leave it alone. Before work or before my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or rubbing the patch on your face is something you feel compelled to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t really want to do it, but it feels like I have to. Before work, or if I’m going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at that spot. And if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or start making an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or rub the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I feel like if I don’t check it, I might go out and everyone will notice it. Like before work or my sister’s game night, I keep looking in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under the light, rubbing at it. If it seems too obvious, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just come up with a reason not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the patch on your face is a ritual you have to complete before you can feel okay about going out?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is. Before I go to work or over to my sister’s game night, I end up in the bathroom checking it over and over, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. I don’t really feel okay leaving until I’ve done that. And if it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check the patch on your face if you didn't think it looked bad?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much, no. It’s really when I think it looks bad or too obvious that I get stuck checking it. Like before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the mirror, turn my face in different lights, rub at the spot… and if I still think it shows, I’ll wear my hat low or make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check the patch on your face driven more by the possibility that it might look bad to others, or by an internal feeling that you just have to check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more about thinking it might look bad to other people. Like, before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking it in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at it. If I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check the patch on your face driven by the fact that you think it's actually flawed, or is it more that you just feel compelled to check it regardless of how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s because I think it’s really flawed. I see this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch on my face, especially in photos or in the mirror, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up. People tell me they barely notice it, but I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check the patch on your face if someone you trust told you it looked perfectly fine?\n\nPatient: Even if someone I trust said it looked fine, I’d probably still check it. I might feel better for a minute, but before work or before going to my sister’s game night, I’d end up back in the bathroom mirror, turning my face in the light and rubbing at the spot. If it still looked too obvious to me, I’d wear my hat low or sometimes just not go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is actually flawed, or do you just feel compelled to keep checking it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s actually flawed. I mean, I know people tell me they barely notice anything, but when I look in the mirror or see it in photos, I see this rough, kind of sunken patch, and it makes that side of my face look old and beat-up. That’s what keeps pulling me back to check it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to check the patch on your face if you were completely alone and nobody would see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would still check it. Even if nobody was going to see me, I’d still end up looking in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights and rubbing at that spot. It’s like I need to see how bad it looks to me. Before work or going somewhere, like my sister’s game night, it gets worse, and if it looks too obvious I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check the patch is to fix or improve how it looks, or is it just something you feel you must do?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but mostly it feels like I have to do it. Before work, or if I’m going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, turning my face in different light and rubbing at the spot like maybe I can make it look better. And if I decide it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the urge to check the patch on your face even if you were convinced it looked perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. If I really believed it looked normal, maybe the urge wouldn’t be as strong. But what usually happens is before work or going to my sister’s game night, I end up in the bathroom checking it anyway, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at that spot. And if I think it stands out too much, I’ll pull my hat low or just not go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s on my mind a lot. I keep catching it in the mirror or thinking about how it looked in a photo, that rough, kind of sunken patch. To me it makes one side of my face look old and beat-up, even though people say they barely notice it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the patch on your face interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. I’ve been late getting out the door for construction jobs because I get stuck checking it or trying to make it look less noticeable. And I’ve started avoiding site photos. I’ve also skipped some family game nights because I just don’t want people looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern only about the appearance of this patch on your face or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s really just that patch on my face. I don’t spend much time worrying about other parts of my body. It’s that rough, sunken-looking spot that I keep seeing in the mirror and in photos, like it makes one side of my face look old or beat-up, even though people tell me they barely notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repetitive, like touching or picking at the patch on your face, in response to your concerns about its appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Before work, or before going to my sister’s game night, I’ll get stuck checking it in the bathroom mirror. I turn my face different ways under the light to see how bad it looks. I’ll rub at the spot too, like that’s going to change anything. Sometimes I wear my hat low, or if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior related to the patch on your face is causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, it’s not just bothering me in my head — it’s making me late leaving for construction jobs, and I’ve started dodging site photos. I’ve even skipped some family game nights because I don’t want everyone looking at my face. So yeah, it’s causing me stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or rubbing the patch on your face if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I tell myself I’m just going to look once, but then I end up turning my face under the light, checking it again, rubbing at the spot. Especially before work or going to my sister’s game night. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or start thinking of a reason not to go. It’s hard to just stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking or rubbing the patch on your face in response to an anxiety or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s pretty much it. I get this anxious feeling like I have to look at it, like I can’t just leave it alone. Before work or before my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. If it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or rubbing the patch on your face is something you feel compelled to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t really want to do it, but it feels like I have to. Before work, or if I’m going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at that spot. And if I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or start making an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or rub the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I feel like if I don’t check it, I might go out and everyone will notice it. Like before work or my sister’s game night, I keep looking in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under the light, rubbing at it. If it seems too obvious, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just come up with a reason not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the patch on your face is a ritual you have to complete before you can feel okay about going out?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is. Before I go to work or over to my sister’s game night, I end up in the bathroom checking it over and over, turning my face in different light, rubbing at the spot. I don’t really feel okay leaving until I’ve done that. And if it looks too obvious to me, I’ll pull my hat down low or sometimes just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check the patch on your face if you didn't think it looked bad?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much, no. It’s really when I think it looks bad or too obvious that I get stuck checking it. Like before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the mirror, turn my face in different lights, rub at the spot… and if I still think it shows, I’ll wear my hat low or make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check the patch on your face driven more by the possibility that it might look bad to others, or by an internal feeling that you just have to check it?\n\nPatient: It’s more about thinking it might look bad to other people. Like, before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep checking it in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at it. If I think it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check the patch on your face driven by the fact that you think it's actually flawed, or is it more that you just feel compelled to check it regardless of how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s because I think it’s really flawed. I see this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch on my face, especially in photos or in the mirror, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up. People tell me they barely notice it, but I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check the patch on your face if someone you trust told you it looked perfectly fine?\n\nPatient: Even if someone I trust said it looked fine, I’d probably still check it. I might feel better for a minute, but before work or before going to my sister’s game night, I’d end up back in the bathroom mirror, turning my face in the light and rubbing at the spot. If it still looked too obvious to me, I’d wear my hat low or sometimes just not go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is actually flawed, or do you just feel compelled to keep checking it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s actually flawed. I mean, I know people tell me they barely notice anything, but when I look in the mirror or see it in photos, I see this rough, kind of sunken patch, and it makes that side of my face look old and beat-up. That’s what keeps pulling me back to check it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to check the patch on your face if you were completely alone and nobody would see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would still check it. I see this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch on my face, especially in photos or in the mirror, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up. People tell me they barely notice it, but I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check the patch on your face if someone you trust told you it looked perfectly fine?\n\nPatient: Even if someone I trust said it looked fine, I’d probably still check it. I might feel better for a minute, but before work or before going to my sister’s game night, I’d end up back in the bathroom mirror, turning my face in the light and rubbing at the spot. If it still looked too obvious to me, I’d wear my hat low or sometimes just not go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is actually flawed, or do you just feel compelled to keep checking it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s actually flawed. I mean, I know people tell me they barely notice anything, but when I look in the mirror or see it in photos, I see this rough, kind of sunken patch, and it makes that side of my face look old and beat-up. That’s what keeps pulling me back to check it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to check the patch on your face if you were completely alone and nobody would see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would still check it. Even if nobody was going to see me, I’d still end up looking in the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights and rubbing at that spot. It’s like I need to see how bad it looks to me. Before work or going somewhere, like my sister’s game night, it gets worse, and if it looks too obvious I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check the patch is to fix or improve how it looks, or is it just something you feel you must do?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but mostly it feels like I have to do it. Before work, or if I’m going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, turning my face in different light and rubbing at the spot like maybe I can make it look better. And if I decide it looks too obvious, I’ll wear my hat low or just make an excuse not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the urge to check the patch on your face even if you were convinced it looked perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. If I really believed it looked normal, maybe the urge wouldn’t be as strong. But what usually happens is before work or going to my sister’s game night, I end up in the bathroom checking it anyway, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at that spot. And if I think it stands out too much, I’ll pull my hat low or just not go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check the patch on your face something that you feel is driven by the flaw itself or by a feeling inside you that you must check it?\n\nPatient: It feels like both, but more like something inside me that says I have to check it. The patch sets it off, I guess, but then I get stuck with it. Before work or going to my sister’s game night, I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, turning my face under different lights, rubbing at the spot.
